docs/ein/user/04-reference/02-syntax.md:15:| `NAME` | ``[A-Za-z0-9_.\-$%^*+=<>!&@~/'`\[\]{}]+`` | `has-color`, `House-1`, `is-a*`, `7`, `-7`, `1..5`, `=`, `<=`, `a/b`, `f'` | the name of an atom: an argument, a relation, a rule's name, a slot's label, a step. **The name rule**: a run of ASCII name characters, each name printed as written and reloading to itself, so `7` and `07` are two names. The guard is monotone (no nested absent), so the\n; candidate would be retired on its first judgement: a guard that never passes.\n;\n; The grammar refuses `?x` there at the parse, as a premise that is not a\n; form, before any compiler sees it: a variable stands for a value, and\n; `absent` reads a premise. This shape's class is a rule\n; shape that reads nothing, in silence; the guard this one may mean is\n; written `(absent (p ?x))`.\n(relation p Thing)\n\n(rule vacuous-guard ()\n  :match  (and (p ?x) (absent ?x))\n  :assert (p ?x))\n"

Each states its claim, which the text does not move. **`16_eq_neq_goal.ein` asks about `eq` and `neq` in a goal**: they are relations, computed and never stored, a variable nothing else binds ranging over the program's names, and each of its nine queries states its class; **`17_eq_neq_premise.ein`** writes them in a rule's premise: a guard before the premise that binds its variable, a fact as a guard's argument and a guard whose variable no premise binds, four claims about root |
examples/README.md:127:| [`arith/`](arith/) | **the calculator**: programs over `std.math.*`, arithmetic written in Ein over digit tables and computed by saturation alone, with no search: the universe has no candidate, R is {∅}, and `ein solve`, `ein saturate` and `ein test` read the same answer (`ein-cli/tests/arith_search_free.rs` holds it of every file here). A number is `z` or `(n PREFIX DIGIT)`, in reading order; a request fact `(add X Y)` is answered by `(sum-of X Y S)`. `add.ein`, `sub.ein`, `mul.ein` and `div.ein` are the four operations of `std.math.decimal-arith`, each with a rule of the program's own that reads an answer or chains a request from it; `ein solve examples/arith/add.ein --trace T.md` writes the addition's steps as sentences, the check of its operands among them (a request the theory cannot read, `(add (n 1 2) X)` or `(add foo X)` with no `(let foo …)`, is flagged, `(malformed REQUEST)` or `(undeclared NAME REQUEST)`, and the flag derives `(false)`: [`stdlib/README.md`](../stdlib/README.md) says what is flagged and where). `div_by_zero.ein` is a division by zero: the request and its tagged call each derive the flag `(division-by-zero …)`, the flag derives `(false)`, root is dead, and `ein solve` names the request and its two `let`s as the core; it is the one file here whose R is not {∅}. **Three files are generated**, each a different way a program can be long: `long_numbers.ein` (few requests on operands of up to 100 digits), `many_calculations.ein` (100 requests on short numbers) and `factorial.ein` (a chain from one written fact, 25! by three rules of the program's own, which stops by itself where the comparison answers `eq`); `ein saturate` prints the time of each phase. `num9.ein`, `num9_overflow.ein` and `num9_both_ways.ein` are `std.math.num9`'s, nine digits in one fact computed by wide rules, an overflow flagged `(overflow REQUEST)`, and both modules in one program through the conversion requests. `int.ein` is `std.math.int`'s, an integer `(int P N)` with its value P − N, the three division conventions side by side; `rat.ein` is `std.math.rat`'s, a rational `(rat Z D)`, an answer unreduced and `rat-simplify` asked for lowest terms; `gcd.ein` is `std.math.gcd`'s, the largest square tile for a floor and two buses that leave together again |
examples/README.md:128:| [`beyond_arith/`](beyond_arith/) | **what the calculator's tables do besides arithmetic** ([`08-word-problems.md`](../docs/ein/user/02-guide/08-word-problems.md) says what each shows). Eighteen programs, each with a claim, beside `arith/` and not in it because five of them **search**. **Tables read backwards, by saturation alone:** `inverse_addition.ein` (x + 4567 = 127645 gives x = 123078 with nothing guessed), `tables_backwards.ein` (a column with two answers, the next column ending the wrong one), `equation_refuted.ein` (x × 7 = 862 refuted at root, whose core is the proof that it has no solution). **Equations by search,** the unknown's digits as hypotheses under `total-owed` and `functional`: `equation_by_search.ein`, `equation_two_unknowns.ein` (3x + 5y = 47, three states), `equation_no_solution.ein` (R empty because every alive node owes, its claim the owing frontier), `cryptarithm.ein` (I + BB = ILL), `send_more_money.ein` (SEND + MORE = MONEY, its claim held by `ein test`, which asks `--single`'s question) and `send_more_money_written.ein`, **generated** from it and not edited by hand, the same program with its letters declared as the puzzle is written: one universe and one answer, and `--single` doing the same work on both, since the obligation tree orders its branches by the columns it reads off the program and not by the declaration. **Bounded theorem checking:** `laws.ein` (commutativity, associativity, distributivity and the division identity on every instance over 0 to 3) and `column_lemma.ein` (each of the full adder's 200 rows against addition derived by counting). **Number theory by saturation,** each a chain of `std.math.decimal-arith`'s tagged calls: `primes.ein`, `gcd.ein` (importing `std.math.gcd`), `casting_out_nines.ein`, `perfect.ein`, `collatz.ein` (27 stopped at its bound), `pythagorean.ein` and `fibonacci.ein` |

stdlib/README.md:381:| **the number** | `z`, zero, or `(n PREFIX DIGIT)`, ten times the prefix plus the digit, so it reads as written: 42 is `(n (n z 4) 2)`. Any length: no width, no overflow. The normal form has no high zero; an operand written with high zeros is read by value, and every answer is in normal form |
stdlib/README.md:383:| **subtraction** | `(sub X Y)` is answered by `(diff-of X Y D R)`, **two numbers**: D is X − Y where X is not the smaller and `z` otherwise, and R is what X is short by, Y − X, where X is the smaller and `z` otherwise. One of the two is always `z`, both when X = Y. The theory has no negative number, and R is its place: a rule that matches `(diff-of X Y z (n ?p ?a))` holds exactly where X is the smaller, with no negation written |
stdlib/README.md:387:| **division by zero** | a contradiction, **in two steps**: a request whose divisor is zero, however written, derives the flag `(division-by-zero (div X Y))`, and the flag derives `(false)` by `std.math.arith`'s rule ([Q-M1f.269](../docs/decisions/stdlib/math/Q-M1f.269-division-by-zero-is-a-contradiction.md)). Root is dead, R is empty, and no query of the program has an answer; `ein solve` names the request in the unsat core and `division-by-zero-is-false` as what refuted it. A program that must not die compares its divisor with zero first and derives its `div` request by a rule over `(cmp-of Y z gt)`. **It is flagged wherever it is called** ([Q-M1f.163](../docs/decisions/stdlib/math/decimal/Q-M1f.163-a-division-by-zero-is-flagged-wherever-division.md)): every division goes through the tagged call `(div-call TAG X Y)`, and a call whose Y is zero by value derives `(division-by-zero (div-call TAG X Y))`, the call as it was asserted, and `(false)` from it, whoever made the call, a rule of a program's own included. A public request therefore holds two flags, its own and its call's under the tag `(quotient X Y)` ([Q-M1f.211](../docs/decisions/stdlib/math/decimal/Q-M1f.211-a-public-division-by-zero-holds-two-flags.md)); `ein solve`'s read-out names the request, and `--trace` and `ein saturate --dump` show the second flag. `std.math.int`, `std.math.rat` and `std.math.num9` see a zero divisor before they call, so theirs is one flag, the request's. **A theory in which division by zero is allowed** (a wheel, a meadow, transreal arithmetic) would be a module of its own that does not import `std.math.arith`; none is built |
stdlib/README.md:388:| **named constants** | `(let NAME NUMBER)` names a number, and a request may name an operand: `(add price qty)` is answered by `(sum-of price qty S)` ([Q-M1f.149](../docs/decisions/stdlib/math/decimal/Q-M1f.149-a-request-may-name-an-operand-by-a-let.md)). The same sum written with the numbers is another request with its own answer. A name stands for one number: a name no `let` states, a name stated twice with two numbers, and `z` or a digit as a name are each flagged (below) |
stdlib/README.md:389:| **a malformed request** | a contradiction, **in two steps**, as a division by zero is ([Q-M1f.316](../docs/decisions/stdlib/math/Q-M1f.316-the-calculator-flags-each-malformed-request-it.md)). `(malformed REQUEST)`, the request as written, where a digit stands for an operand; `(malformed (let NAME NUMBER))` where a `let` cannot be read; `(undeclared NAME REQUEST)` where the request names an operand no `let` states. A malformed **numeral** is `std.math.decimal`'s, `(not-decimal NUMERAL THING)`, flagged where the request's operand is looked up; a numeral no request takes is not checked ([Q-M1f.404](../docs/decisions/stdlib/math/Q-M1f.404-a-numeral-is-checked-where-an-operation-takes-it.md)). Each flag derives `(false)` by a rule of its own. Root is dead, R is empty, every command exits 0, and `ein solve` names the request that holds the fault, in the unsat core. **A flag means malformed; no flag does not mean well-formed** ([§ Malformed requests and numerals](#malformed-requests-and-numerals-what-is-flagged-where-and-what-stays-silent)) |

stdlib/README.md:914:| **conversions** | a request each way, one firing: `(to9 X)`, X a natural number of `std.math.decimal`, is answered by `(as9 X W)`, or by `(big X)` where X is written with more than nine digits; `(from9 W)` by `(of9 W X)`, X in normal form and labelled `(valid X)` as it is made. The number `to9` takes is asked of `std.math.decimal`'s check, `(check X X)`, which flags a malformed one ([Q-M1f.404](../docs/decisions/stdlib/math/Q-M1f.404-a-numeral-is-checked-where-an-operation-takes-it.md)). stdlib/README.md:1013:| **the integer** | `(int P N)`. 7 is `(int (n z 7) z)`, −7 is `(int z (n z 7))`, zero is `(int z z)`, and `(int (n z 5) (n z 3))` is 2, not simplified. The **normal form** has one part `z` and the other in `std.math.decimal-arith`'s normal form; every integer has exactly one. utils/README.md:50:| [`review_bundle.py`](review_bundle.py) | **M1e2's review bundle** (P1e2.1). With no arguments it writes `build/review/`: an index and **one page per row of [`plans/wrong_answers/register.toml`](../plans/wrong_answers/register.toml)**, the open wrong answers (a fixed one is a regression of the project's `corpus/regressions.toml`, which carries no record to review). Each page runs the row's `[case.now]` — the region question the register holds it to — and sets it beside the walk's wrong answer, control and twins (the register's record of them: the walk has no CLI path since the switch) and the right answer, diffs the states with `ein solve --diff-models`, says whether the run still answers what `[case.now]` records, draws the run on R in **§ The region** (S1e2.1a.7: `ein oracle --dump`'s cube with the run's `--dump-lattice` and `--dump-answer` on it, `ein render region --visited --answer`; the oracle's lines beside the register's `[case.region]` and `right` and the run's states; what the drawing found missing, spurious or read otherwise than the cube, each linked to its node's row; one line and the run alone past the oracle's limit), says what fixed a fixed row (its `[case.fixed]`, and whether its `wrong` was the walk's or a region run's), links the row's `look_at` pointers into the run (a walk-only one says why it is absent), and ends with the review's checklist. A row id or case (`W1`) builds a subset, a `.ein` path gets a page of its own (a bare `solve` unless flags follow `--`), and `--corpus` builds one per corpus entry at its first declared `solve` run. `--corpus` and `--regions` program pages carry the same § The region. `--smoke` is a **per-commit gate step**: one small case and one program whose solve is refused (M1e2 S1e2.1.7: a page that links a dump the run did not write dangles), link-checked; since M1f S1f.5.19 also two cases whose run is not a solve (`W40/kb-save`, `W41/saturate-import`; since the register split, S1f.20.9, the smoke's cases are open rows, `W39/or-one-key` and `W87/written-rule-no-such-rule`, a command's, and the container and closed-stdout shapes are fixed rows no open row has), and since S1f.5.25 one whose stdout is closed (`W75/solve`, the register's schema /21), run with its reader closed before the run and read by how it ended, every DOT it kept held to its SVG (since S1f.5.29 in the whole bundle too: a DOT Graphviz refused exits 1, with what Graphviz said printed under its path; a lattice past 1 000 nodes is kept undrawn by design and says so; `--smoke --keep DIR` keeps the smoke's build), and first **every register row read with no run** through the index's cells and each case page's register-only parts, so a row shape the bundle cannot read fails the commit that files it. **A row whose `[case.now]` is a command's run that is not a solve** (the register's schema /16: `saturate`, a `render` view, `kb save`) has a case page of its own ([`review/command.py`](review/command.py)): the command as run, its exit, stdout and stderr in full (inline and as files), the register's `wrong`, `now` and `right` beside it with whether the run answers `now` as the project's runner (`ein-cli/tests/held/`) reads it (with a container, the same run on the witness, compared), and every artefact it writes, linked and drawn; a `container` row, a solve's too, runs on the witness saved as a `.einb`. The whole bundle was the nightly's until M1f S1f.5.27 and is the dev host's since (`./run_tests.sh --dev-host`, or `utils/ci_container.sh --dev-host` on the runner's Graphviz): 175 cases, 6 739 pages, 3 948 SVGs, 3.1 GB, 356 s (2026-11-09 at `00c4ed34`, the nightly's step, `python3 utils/review_bundle.py`; `find build/review -name '*.md' | wc -l`, `-name '*.svg'`, `du -sh build/review`), where it was 35 cases, 10 380 pages, 1 709 SVGs, 611 MB and 97 s on 2026-11-01 at `17422b32`; the gallery, 17 program pages, 22 min. `run FILE [FLAGS…] --out DIR` writes **one run, fully explicit**: every artefact `ein solve` can write for it, and `ein oracle --dump`'s cube beside it, kept verbatim under `raw/` and rendered under `pages/`. That is the program, root, the region trace by section with every graph an SVG, every node the run visited (`--dump-lattice`, a page per step, and `ein render lattice`'s DAGs, laid out up to 1 000 nodes), the region (`ein oracle --dump` drawn by `ein render region` with this run on it, every node by layer with an anchor per node — M1e2 S1e2.1a.2, S1e2.1a.7), the answer (each state file, its grid and its drawing against root — the first 100, the rest a paged table of links), N, the summary with every counter's defined meaning, the event stream by kind, and seven cross-checks: the artefacts against each other, the run's states against the oracle's on the line it asked, and (7) what `ein render region` found when it drew the run on the cube. The modules are [`review/`](review/__init__.py). A test fails when `ein solve` gains a path-valued flag the bundle does not drive (`help_surface.rs`) | `ein`, Graphviz |

utils/README.md:82:| [`region_synth.py`](region_synth.py) | **the opposite of `region.py`: a program for a chosen region** — M1e2 S1e2.1a.8. A target (R as intervals minus holes, the rest owing or dead, optional implications) is cut into prime implicants, each a `(false)` rule or an `(open h)` obligation with witnesses, and written with the target in the program's header. Since M1e2 S1e2.2.5 a dead node with a non-dead superset is written owing, so a `(false)` rule reads no `absent` and the program is not refused by *forbid*: every hole it writes is owing. `--check` holds the binary's oracle to the header node for node and the static check to the target's monotonicity; `--random N` does that for drawn targets (2 100 checked 2026-10-15, 0 mismatches); `--solution` writes each line node as a loadable program; `--example` / `--list` are the banked shapes in `examples/lattice/synth/`. A dead root under a non-empty R is refused by name: P1e2.1b's. The gate runs `--random 300 --seed 1 --max-n 6` ([`check_region_routes.py`](check_region_routes.py)), and `--random 200 --seed 5 --max-n 6 --write` is the detector's fixture ([`gen_random_programs.py`](gen_random_programs.py)) | `ein` |
utils/README.md:87:| [`gen_arith_examples.py`](gen_arith_examples.py) | **writes** the three generated examples of `examples/arith/`, `long_numbers.ein`, `many_calculations.ein` and `factorial.ein` (M1f S1f.6.15): operands drawn with a fixed seed, every claimed answer computed by Python's integers, which are the oracle for the files and enter no program. `--check` exits 1 when a file is not what the script writes; `--out DIR --digits N --short N --requests N --operand N --factorial N` writes other sizes elsewhere, and `arith_cost.py` calls its three functions for its sweep. **One program of a stated size, on demand** (M1f S1f.6.29, the user's *"make a generator in python that takes some size arguments"*): `--op add|sub|cmp|mul|div|mix|factorial --digits N [--dividend N] [--count N] [--n N] [--seed S] [--no-expect] -o FILE`, the same arguments the same bytes; a claim is Python's answer, left out where it would nest past the parser's limit of 1 000, and an operand past 999 digits is refused.
